894.8591/21
I have the honor to refer to the Department's
ciroular telegraphic instruction dated December 17,
894.8591
894.3591/84
1940, 9 p.m., requiring consular officers to submit
telegraphic reports to the Department in confidential
code when bills of health are issued to Japanese
merchant vessels of 4,000 gross tons or over and to
NAVY
alf naval vessels and whenever information is
SENT TO
E
AN27 1941
mublicly available on sailings of such vessels for
estinations other than the United States.
EEBI1 9 1941
ASE
FILED
COPY
This despatch is prepared to inform the
Dopartment of the situation with respect to compliance
with the Department's instruction by American consular
officers in Australia. Owing to the requirements of
PSGC
the Commonwealth Defence Department applidations
STATE
for bills of health do not include full intormation
regarding the itinerary of vessels. Regular sailings
19941
of Japanese merchant vessels for several years have
wy
not

=== Page 184 === (confidence 0.98)
-2-
not included sailings from Sydney for ports in the
United States or for the Canal Zone. They inolude
rogular sailings for Far Eastern ports. There is a
reguler monthly service of vessels of the N.Y.K. Line
between Australia and ports in the Far East which
usually inoludes calls at Manila and Davao in the
Philippine Islands beth on the voyage southward and
on the return voyage to Japan.
Paraphrases of the Department's instruotion
were mailed on December 20 to the American Legation,
Canberra, for its information, and to the Consulates
at Melbourne, Brisbane, Adelaide, and Perth. It
is the understanding of this office, and the other
Consulates in Australia have been instructed accord-
ingly, that when a vessel of Japanese registry applies
for a bill of health at more than one port in Australia
it is not necessary for any office other than the one
at which an application for a bill of health is first
made to send a report to the Department unless there
is a change in the vessel's itinerary or special
ciroumstances call for another report. Each office
in Australia will mail paraphrases of all such reports
which it prepares to the consular offices at ports in
Australia at which the vessel is likely to call on
the same voyage. The other Consulates in Australia
have been asked to send to this office in each instance
a copy of each report which they send to the Department
under this instruotion.
Information for bills of health regarding ports visited
withheld  r mreer of Commonwealth Defenoe Department
The Department:s ciroular telegraphio instruotion
dirooted

=== Page 185 === (confidence 0.98)
directed consular officers preparing reports required
by the instruction to include information  showing the
last port of call at which the vessel called and the
ports to which the vessel is sailing. This Consulate
General's despatch number 543/dated September 14, 1939,
1921683
(Consular File No. 8l2.2) entitled "Effect of Common-
wealth Defense Regulations upon Bills of Health" reported
that the Commonwealth Department of Defence had instructed
masters and steamship agents not to supply in applications
for bills of health information with regard to previous
ports visited or dates or ports of destination other
than the port for which the bill of health is required.
Since early in September 1939, the Consulate General
has been unable to include in bills of health which it
has issued the names of ports visited preceding arrival
at Sydney, dates and hours of arrival and departure at
Sydney, and names of ports at which the vessel will
stop before reaching the port for which the bill of
health is issued. Reference is made in the blank spaces
for this information to a footnote reading as follows:
"This information withheld by order of
the Commonwealth of Australia Department of Defence."
Unless the Department of Defence is prepared to
allow additional details concerning vessels which apply
for bills of health to be supplied to American consular
officers in Australia, it appears that it will in most
instances be impossible for the consular officers in
Australia to supply the full information desired in
the telographio reports required by the Department's
ciroular instruction of December 17, 9 p.m.
2

=== Page 186 === (confidence 0.99)
In mailing a copy of the Department's ciroular
tolegraphio instrustion to the Legation, the foregoing
eireumstances were brought to its attention and a
copy of the Consulate General's despatch number 545
of September 14, 1939, was forwarded with the
Consulate General's letter.
Sailings of Japanese merchant vessels from Sydney
According to the Consulate General's present
information, there are three regular Japanese
steamship services operating between eastern
Australian ports and Japanese ports.
The N.Y.K. (Japan Mail) Line vessels Kamo Maru,
Atuta Maru and Kitano Maru sail once every five weeks
from Sydney and are underatood to call at Brisbane,
Thursday Island, Davao and Manila, en route to Japan.
Bofore calling at Sydney on the homeward voyage the
preceding ports of call during l939 were usually
listed on bills of health issued by this Consulate
General as follows: Japanese ports (Yokohama,
Magoya, Osaka, Kobe, Mozi, Miike, Nagasaki), Hong
Kong, Manila, Davao, Thursday Island, Brisbane,
Sydney, and Melbourne. In April 1940, the Suwa Maru
called in at Sydney, presumably on the same voyage
since it was bound for Davao and Manila, in place
of the Kamo Maru. An advertisement in the "Daily
Commeroial News and Shipping List" of December 24,
1940, indicates that the Suwa Maru will again be
employed in this service.
The O.S.K. Line vessels Sydney Maru, Canberra Maru,
and

=== Page 187 === (confidence 0.98)
Tekyo Maru sail from Sydney on a regular monthly
viee for Kobe, Osaka, Nagoya, and Yokohama. In
Poh 19s9, the agents of the Sydney Maru applied
fes = bill of health at this office and it was
rocorded that this vessel was to sail from Sydney direet
to Cebu, Philippine Islands, on March 18, 1939.
In May 1939, application was made at this office
for a bill of health for the Tokyo Maru and it
was noted that this vessel was likewise to sail
from Sydney direct to Cebu. Since then no bills
of health for vessels of this line have been applied
for. In the bills of health issued to the Sydney
Maru  in March l939, preceding ports of call were
listed as follows: Japanese ports (Otaru, Hakodate,
Yokohama, Nagoya, Osaka, Kobe, Moji),Rabaul (1n
New Guinea), Australian ports (Brisbane, Sydney,
and Melbourne), New Zealand ports (Bluff, Dunedin,
Lyttelton, Wellington, and Auckland). In the
bill of health issued in May 1939 to the Tokyo
Maru, the itinerary was the same except that Otaru
and Hakodate in Japan were not included and the
port of Napier was visited in addition to the
other New Zealand ports.
The following vessels of the Japan Australia
Line have been advertised at different times in the
Sydney "Daily Commeroial News and Shipping List" as
engaged in a direct monthly service to Japan from
Molbourne, Sydney, and Brisbane, with a bi-monthly
service to Adelaide, and as prepared to accept cargo
for transhipment to all Japanese and Chinese ports:
Kegve Mara, Morfolk Maru, China Maru, Kosin Maru,
named

=== Page 188 === (confidence 0.98)
-6 -
named are advertised as ourrently engaged in this
service.
Since the sailing of the O.S.k. Line vessel
Tokyo Maru from Sydney on May 17, 1940, applications
for bills of health for vessels of Japanese registry
have been made at this office solely for the vessels
of the N.Y.K. Line prior to their sailings from Sydney
for Brisbane, Thursday Island, Davao, and Manila. (It
is assumed that these vessels usually call at Brisbane
and Thursday Island before calling at Davao and Manila
because those two places were regular ports of call
listed on bills of health for these vessels before
September 1939).
It appears that occasions for reporting sailings
of Japanese vessels for ports in the United States or
for Japanese vessels scheduled to call at ports in
the Panama Canal Zone will seldom arise at American
consular offices in Australia. Reports on sailings
of Japanese vessels for ports in the Philippine
Islands will be reported in accordance with the
Department's instruction.
Respectfully yours
Albert M. Doyle

M.V. SYDNEY MARU
In addition to reporting that this information
will henceforth be sent regularly from Auckland at
Mr. Pinkerton's direction, I quote also at his
suggestion, information obtained from the Harbor
Master of Auckland in an informal talk to the effect
that the lines above mentioned are the only ones now
calling at this port, that the tramps and other
chartered vessels that formerly entered Auckland at
irregular intervals no longer call at this port.
Respectfully yours,
(J.G.Groeninger)

889
SUBJECT:
Visits of Japanese Vessels to French Oceania
COPY made
THE HONORABLE
arSENT TO
NAVY
THE SECRETARY OF STATE,
FEB 2 6 1941
WASHINGTON.
U - LIAISON OFFICE
SIR:
I have the honor to inform the Department that at
894.8591/175
at the present time there are three Japanese cargo vessels
in the waters of French Oceania, two at Makatea Island
894.8591
and one at Papeete, whioh have called ostensibly for the
purpose of loading cargos of phosphate at Makated for
shipment to Japan.
It is understood that the vessel which is now in
the harbor of Papeete entered for the reason that there
is no anchorage at Makatea for more than two vessels at
the same time. It will proceed there after one of the
other vessels has completed loading and sailed it is
Conidr cl File
reported.
Now
This matter is being reported because of the fact
IAIN OGCE
STATE
that it is unusual for more than one vessel to call at
Confidential File
Makatea at the same time and because formerly this
trade was carried on almost exolusivly by British vessels.
That
MAR 4 - 1.941

=== Page 375 === (confidence 0.96)
-2-
That three Japanese vessels should be there at the same
time has caused considerable comment.
Japan takes practically the entire output of phos-
phate from Makatea. During the calandar year 1939, the
last year for which statistics are available, Japan took
136,763 metric tons of the 160,680 metric tons erported.
It is reported that Japen took the entire production
during 1940, with the exseption of about 6,000 tons
shipped to Honolulu.
Whether or not there is any significance in the
three vessels being here at the same time it is difficult
to say, however, in view of the present international
situation and the recent raid on Naru Island, whick has
normally supplied much of Japan's phosphatr requirements,
it seems strange that they should arrive at this time.
Very respectfully,
Goage E EFS
George C. Cobb
Amerigan vice Consul

PARAPHRASE
Telegram no. 337
Dated: February 28, 1941
10 a.m
From: Embassy, Tokyo.
Rec'd: February 28, 1la.m.
A telegram has been received by the Embassy from
the Consul at Manila conveying a request from the
Military Intelligence which desires to be furnished with
repetitions of cables sent in brown code to the Depart-
ment via Shanghai in which movements of Japanese vessels
are reported by consuls stationed in Japan.
I request instructions as to whether the telegrams
on this matter sent by the consuls should be forwarded
to Manila. If my assumption is correct please state
what code must be used and to whom the messages shall
be addressed.
GREW
894.8591/199

Copy to Wary, 3/6/41.

WASHINGTON.
U - LIAISON OFFICE A
SIR:
I have the honor to refer to the Department's
telegraphic instruction of December 17, 9 p.m.(1940)
to the Consulate General at Wellington and to the
Consulate's despatch No. 152 of January 15,1941,
transmitting the first of this series of reports
PS/FF
and to state that as of possible further interest
the following change in the schedule of one of the
vessels is reported herewith.
Up until as late as February 12,194l the Osaka
Syosen Kaisya's motor vessel SYDNEY MARU was advertised
to sail from Auckland for Australian ports and thence
to Japan. Yesterday's announcement however, stated
that the SYDNEY MARU had arrived in Auckland and would
sail direct for Japan. Additional local information
is to the effect that there is very little cargo for
these vessels now and that they probably are operating
at a loss.
Respectfully yours,
T Momngn
MAR 1 9 1941

=== Page 518 === (confidence 0.98)
APRYV1941
MENTOFSTATE
FAREASTERN AFFAIRS
wny
PR161841
April 16, 1941
IN
PA/H
DEPARTMENT OF ATATE
Reference Singapore's telegram no 42,
oh 19, 1941.
It is noted that the American Consul
ral at Singapore reports that the Minis-
of Shipping at London has instructed the
Sangapore authorities to limit supplies to
Japanese vessels of water, coal and oil to
Buch quantities as may be necessary for such
vessels to reach their next port of call; that
the Consul General has been informed by the
Singapore authorities that the Netherlands
authorities have expressed willingness to
cooperate; that the purpose is to prevent the
supplying of enemy raiders; and that it is the
view of the Singapore authorities that such
efforts will be seriously hindered unless a
similar practice is adopted in the Philippine
Islands.
FE understands that there has been dis-
cussion between the Under Secretary's office,
RA, EA, the Navy (Captain Shuirman), the Army,
and representatives of oil companies with
regard to limiting the fueling of German and
Italian ships in the western hemisphere. It
is understood that from these discussions
there has resulted a practice which has been
fairly successfuf in tharsthaormeot of its
purpose, although differences of practice as
between various of the American Rapublics have
resulted in occasionalAGerman and Italian
ships putting to sea with large fuel supplies.
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
It

=== Page 519 === (confidence 0.99)
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
DIVISION OF FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
-2-
It should be noted that the relative effective-
ness of the measures taken has beem derived
from eooperation between the American Republics
and from voluntary cooperation extended by
the oil companies; it should be noted that the
limitation has not been a limitation imposed
by law.
The suggestion of extending a similar
practice to the Far East raises certain
questions of law which warrant careful con-
sideration. There is understood to be basis
in international law and in American legis-
lation (enacted in the early years of the
United States) for limiting the supplying
of belligerent public vessels; but Japan has
not been proclaimed a belligerent, and the
Japanese vessels which would presumably fall
within the scope of the action contemplated
in Singapore's 42 would no doubt be mainly
commercial ships. Public Resolution 54,
76th Congress, approved November 4, 1939,
known as the Neutrality Act of 1939, states
in part in Section 10:
"(a) Whenever, during any war
in which the United States is neutral,
the President, or any person thereunto
authorized by him, shall have cause
to believe that any vessel, domestic
or foreign, whether requiring clearance
or not, is about to carry out of a
port or from the jurisdiction of the
United States, fuel, men, arms, ammuni-
tion, implements of war, supplies,
dispatches

=== Page 520 === (confidence 0.99)
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
DIVISION OF FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
-3-
dispatches, or information to any
warship, tender, or supply ship of a
state named in a proclamation issued
under the authority of section 1 (a),
but the evidence is not deemed sufficient
to justify forbidding the departure of
the vessel as provided for by section
1, title V, chapter 30, of the Act
approved June 15, 1917 (40 Stat. 217,
221; U.S.C., 1934 edition, title 18,
sec. 31), and if, in the President's
judgment, such action will serve to
maintain peace between the United
States and foreign states, or to pro-
tect the commercial interests of the
United States and its citizens, or to
promote the security or neutrality of
the United States, he shall have the
power, and it shall be his duty, to
require the owner, master, or person in
command thereof, before departing from
a port or from the jurisdiction of the
United States, to give a bond to the
United States, with sufficient sureties,
in such amount as he shall deem proper,
conditioned that the vessel will not
deliver the men, or any fuel, supplies,
dispatches, information, or any part of
the cargo, to any warship, tender, or
supply ship of a state named in a
proclamation issued under the authority
of section 1 (a).
(b)

=== Page 521 === (confidence 0.99)
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
DIVISION OF FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
-4-
(b) If the President, or any
person thereunto authorized by him,
shall find that a vessel, domestic or
foreign, in a pert of the United States,
has previously departed from a port
or from the jurisdiction of the United
States during such war and delivered
men, fuel, supplies, dispatches, in-
formation, or any part of its cargo
to a warship, tender, or supply ship of
a state named in a proclamation issued
under the authority of section 1 (a),
he may prohibit the departure of such
vessel during the duration of the war.
The President in a proclamation under the
authority of Section 1 (a), has recognized
that Germany is in a state of war. It is
clear that if the President shall find that
a Japanese vessel has departed from a Philip-
pine port and delivered fuel or supplies to
a German warship, t ender, or supply ship, the
President has authority to prohibit the de-
parture of that vessel during the duration
of the war. On the other hand, there would
appear to be existing no legal basis for
acting on the assumption that all Japanese
vessels are potential suppliers of German
raiders and for accordingly limiting the
fueling and supplying of all Japanese vessels.
If limiting of supplies of water, coal
and oil to Japanese vessels in Philippine
ports to amounts necessary to reach the next
port

=== Page 522 === (confidence 0.97)
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
DIVISION OF FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
-5-
ort of call is to be accomplished not by
oquirement of law but by voluntary cooperation
of the supplying firms in the Philippine
Ialands, careful appraisal of the willingness
of the Philippine authorities and of such
firms to support such an arrangement would
that further steps should not be taken
without consulting the opinion of the High
Commissioner, and it is presumed that PI
may have further comment to offer.
FE assumes that, before decision is
reached, conference would be necessary be-
tween U-L and War and Navy with a view to
considering the practicability of the proposal.
It is assumed that Singapore's 42, March 19,
4 p.m., would not be accepted as final proof
of British and Dutch readiness to cooperate
in the project, but that a direct commitment
by the British and by the Dutch authorities
would be necessary.

THE UNDER SECRETARY
LIAISON OFFICE
April 9, 1941
Dc. Fe18:
You will recall that we decided to
hold this until a decision was reached
about fueling the German and Italian
ips. I understand that this decision

PC
PI does not believe that there is any likelihood
FW 894.8591/245
of commerce raiders being supplied by Japanese vessels
with large amounts of fuel oil from Philippine ports.
It seeme more likely that they would be supplied by
tankers conveying oil from the Netherlands Indies os-
tensibly to Japan or, since fuel oil is not at present
subject to export control, by tankers operating from
Japanese ports. It is not believed that there is any
justification for 1nvoking Section 10 of the Neutrality
Act of 1939 to the extent of assuming that all Japanese
94.8591
vessels are potential suppliers of German raiders and
accordingly requiring a bond or, in normal circumstances,
limiting the supply of bunker fuel oil. This would cer-
tainly seem to be true in the case of Japanese vessels
calling regularly at Philippine ports and taking normi
FILED
supplies of bunker fuel. A Japanese vessel which has not
PS
in the past called regularly at Philippine ports, whi
carries relatively little cargo and purchases a large
amount

=== Page 528 === (confidence 0.99)
-2-
amount of bunker fuel might be regarded with suspicion.
It is doubtful if it will be found necessary to request
the supplying firms in the Philippines to limit their
sales to regular Japanese customers but it would be de-
sirable to obtain the opinion of the High Commissioner on
the proposal and also to endeavor to ascertain whether
there 'are any sales of bunker fuel that might be regarded
as suspicious. It is accordingly suggested that a tele-
gram be sent to the High Commissioner along the following
lines:
Please request all companies in Philippines
selling bunker fuel oil to report to you all sales
to Japanese ships. Inform Department of any such
sales in abnormal quantity or frequency or any
other ciroumstances that might lead to suspicion
that such ships may be supplying commerce raiders.
Please also inform Department whether in your
opinion supplying companies would effectively
cooperate on voluntary basis with proposal to
limit supplies of oil, coal and water to Japa-
nese vessels to amounts necessary to reach next
port of call. Understood such limitation is to
be imposed by authorities in Singapore and
Netherlands Indies. Your comments or suggestions
will be appreciated.
John K. Davis
894.8591/245
PI:RICHARDS:DW

FROM STATE DEPARTMENT FOR OPNAV: AND COAST GUARD:
Following received Mareh 25, 1941, 1:52 p.m. from
Tampico
dated March 25, 11 a.m.
8
On March 24 the Japanese freighter NASOSAN MARU
came in port from New Orleans in ballast and the
TAKETOYO MARU followed her this morning, March 25, from
Tampa, Florida, in ballast also. Both vessels are
unusually large for the port of Tampico and it is
reported that they will return by way of the Panama
Canal to Japan with the striking total of approximately
ten thousand tons of lead. I have telephoned this
information to the Embassy at Mexico City. The Navy
Department should be advised.

=== Page 585 === (confidence 0.98)
PARTMENT OF STATE
Memorandum of Conversation
DATE: Februar
Japanose auxiliary vessel ASAKA MARU.
The British Ambassador, the Right Honorable
the Viscount Halifax;
The Under Secretary, Mr. Welles.
PISTO: S, A-B, PA/D, Eu, PA/H, FE
FEB 26 1941
NOTED
Lord Halifax called to see me this afternoon at his
roquest. He handed me a memorandum dealing with the
Japanese auxiliary vessel ASAKA MARU which has just reached
Lisbon after transiting the Panama Canal. The British
Government stated that itwasinformed that the vessel
would carry on itsreturn journey a cargo of elements of
war of German manufacture and that the British Government
considered that it would be entirely within its rights,
because of the blockade of Germany, in intercepting the
ASAKA MARU on its return voyage to Japan and removing
such part of the cargo as might be of contraband char-
acter. The British Government stated, however, that
rather than do this, it was considering informing the
Japanese Government of its intentions in that regard so

=== Page 586 === (confidence 0.97)
-2-
that the vessel would not load the elements of war at
Lisbon. The British Government urgently desired the
views of the United States.
I told the Ambassador that the matter would be
given immediate consideration and that I would give
him this Government's reply within 24 hours.
2
See also:
German infiltration in North Africa.
U:SW:IJ
